This can result in a shear fracture, dorsal avulsion of the trapezoid, and dorsal dislocation of the index metacarpal or trapezoid.

Palmar dislocations and fracture- dislocations have also been reported. Fewer than 20 isolated fractures of the trapezoid have been reported.

A fracture- dislocation of the trapezoid is usually evident on standard PA, lateral, and oblique radiographic views. Fracture- dislocations of the trapezoid–index metacarpal joint are usually better visualized on the PA view than on the lateral view. A dislocated trapezoid allows proximal migration and overlap of the index metacarpal. Patients present with tenderness at the base of the index metacarpal. Pain can be elicited with motion of the index metacarpal. CT scans may help clarify the injury, and MRI may be helpful for occult fractures.

Isolated fractures of the trapezoid without displacement are usually treated nonoperatively. Displaced fractures are managed by ORIF. If the fragment is small, occasionally closed reduction with cast protection may provide satisfactory results. However, if the fragments are larger, ORIF is recommended. Fractures are stabilized with pin or screw fixation. Excision of the trapezoid is contraindicated secondary to potential proximal migration of the index metacarpal. The trapezoid receives approximately 70% of its interosseous blood supply through dorsal branches. Dorsal dislocations have been complicated by AVN. Highly comminuted or unreconstructible trapezoid fractures and chronic injuries with arthritis are best managed by carpometacarpal arthrodesis, owing to the lack of motion that occurs at the index carpometacarpal joint.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Fractures of the Trapezoid

Incidence
- Trapezoidal fractures are the rarest carpal fractures, at less than 1%

Classiﬁcation
- Type 1 Dorsal rim fracture Type 2 Fracture of the body, often with proximal dorsal subluxation of the second metacarpal

Blood Supply
- 70% dorsal, 30% palmar

Mechanism
- Axial load of index metacarpal Extreme index metacarpal palmar ﬂexion High- energy trauma

Special Radiographs
- CT scanning still often necessary Standard radiographs; CT particularly helpful MRI may be helpful for occult fractures

Treatment
- Nondisplaced fracture: Short- arm thumb spica cast for 4 to 6 weeks Displaced: ORIF with headless compression screws or Kirschner wires; exci sion of fragments is contraindicated because of risk of second metacarpal subluxation Severe comminution with unreconstructible trapezoid: Primary carpometacar pal arthrodesis with possible bone grafting Chronic fracture with arthritic changes: Carpometacarpal arthrodesis

Associated Injury
- Dorsal or palmar dislocation of trapezoid or index metacarpal Multiple injuries of carpal and metacarpal bones, other extremities, and sys tems due to high- energy trauma

Complications
- Delayed union or nonunion Posttraumatic arthritis Possible avascular necrosis if dorsal fracture- dislocation has occurred
```

### Fractures of the Lunate

The lunate is well protected and nearly perfectly concentric with the large lunate fossa of the distal radius. Hence, isolated fractures of the lunate body are rare. Acute traumatic fractures of the lunate account for 1% of all carpal fractures. However, diagnosis and treatment of fractures of the lunate is controversial. It may be difficult to determine whether a fracture of the lunate is acute, traumatic, osteonecrotic (Kienböck disease), or the result of ulnocarpal impaction.

#### Mechanism of Injury and Radiographic Presentation

Fractures of the body of the lunate usually occur from direct axial compression as the head of the capitate is driven proximally into the lunate.

Differences in wrist position may lead to different fracture patterns. The dorsal lip of the lunate may be fractured as the head of the capitate impacts on it and on the dorsal distal edge of the radius in a severe hyperextension and ulnar deviation injury. If the capitate is volarly subluxated on a lateral radiograph, a fracture of the volar lip of the lunate is suspected.

Teisen and Hjarbaek proposed a classification of fresh lunate fractures into five groups based on a review of 17 cases collected over 31 years. These groups are palmar pole fractures with involvement of the palmar nutrient arteries, osteochondral chip fractures of the proximal articular surface without substantial damage to the nutrient vessels, dorsal pole fractures, sagittal fracture through the body of the lunate, and transverse fractures of the body (Fig. 16.52). Fractures may also be comminuted.

![[Fig.16.52.png]]
> **Fig. 16.52** Lateral view line drawings of the five patterns of lunate fractures: palmar pole (I), osteochondral chip (II), dorsal pole (III), sagittal (IV), and transverse (V). (Copyright © Steve K. Lee, MD.)

Patients with lunate fractures present with pain on the dorsum of the wrist and generalized swelling. Fractures of the lunate may be difficult to visualize on standard radiographs because of the overlapping adjacent carpal bones. Careful assessment for carpal instability is imperative because a finding of small lunate fragments either dorsally or palmarly may seem benign by their size but may actually be a sign of major ligamentous disruptions. Imaging should be scrutinized for DISI or VISI deformity, loss of Gilula lines, and carpal gapping. CT is helpful to visualize the fracture fragments and particularly to evaluate for any displacement. MRI may be helpful to evaluate for associated ligamentous injury or vascular impairment.

Fractures of the lunate must be diagnosed and managed promptly. Fractures involving separation of the proximal articular surface from the body of the lunate are usually the result of Kienböck disease. A crescent line parallel to the proximal outline of the lunate is usually seen. Distinguishing a fracture of a normal lunate from pathologic fracture of a lunate with AVN (as in Kienböck disease) must be done.

#### Treatment

Nondisplaced fractures of the lunate with no signs of carpal instability are treated by casting and immobilization, with close follow- up for any displacement. Displaced fractures of the lunate require ORIF, particularly if there are signs of carpal instability. The carpus should be reduced and pinned. Lunate fragments are fixed. If open reduction is required, dorsal and body fractures are approached dorsally, palmar fragments palmarly, and osteochondral fragments either arthroscopically or dorsally.

Dorsal pole fractures occur by shear force transmitted by the capitate and are relatively benign. Treatment is determined according to the clinical findings (pain and swelling) and radiographic findings. Small dorsal fractures are treated with a short course of immobilization with close imaging follow- up, as both the dorsal radiocarpal and dorsal intercarpal ligaments insert on the dorsal lunate (see Chapter 13).

Avulsions of the dorsal portion of the SLIL need to be repaired primarily. Malalignment of the lunocapitate joint (DISI) or scapholunate diastasis is consistent with carpal instability. This portion of the SLIL contains the stout transverse fibers important for stability across the scapholunate interval. A thorough radiographic workup including advanced imaging or arthroscopic inspection must be performed, the carpus needs to be reduced, and the scapholunate ligament needs to be repaired primarily to the lunate (see Chapters 13 and 17).

Fractures of the body of the lunate are approached through a standard dorsal approach via the third dorsal compartments. The EPL is released, the second and fourth dorsal compartments are elevated, and the capsule is opened using a ligament sparing (“window” technique that spares the dorsal intrinsic and extrinsic ligaments; see Chapter 13and Video 13.4). When the radiolunate and/or lunocapitate joint surface is involved, it is best visualized through the dorsal approach. Body fractures may be fixed with headless compression screws and/or Kirschner wires. Comminuted fractures may require a cancellous bone graft harvested from the distal radius. Distraction and temporary scaphocapitate pinning may take pressure off the healing lunate when internal fixation is performed. If there is severe comminution with an unreconstructible lunate or chronic injury with arthritis, proximal row carpectomy or scaphocapitate fusion may be indicated.

VISI may be seen with palmar chip fractures. Palmar lunate fractures are probably the result of a wrist extension injury, with the fragment being avulsed by the short radiolunate ligament. During injury, the hyperextended capitate loads the dorsal portion of the lunate and the lunate rotates dorsally. This causes an avulsion fracture of the palmar lip. If in conjunction with lunotriquetral ligament injury, VISI deformity may be the result.

Volar fragments are reduced through an extended carpal tunnel approach exposing the volar lunate. Because the incision crosses the palmar wrist crease, it is important that the incision be made in a zigzag fashion to avoid flexion contracture from the scar. The incision crosses ulnar to the palmaris longus tendon proximally so as not to affect the palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve. Volar pole fractures are often too small for screw fixation. In these cases, the fragment may be secured with a suture anchor to capture both bone and palmar ligaments. Carpal reduction and stabilization by pinning the scapholunate and lunotriquetral ligaments may be needed. A pin from the scaphoid into the capitate is placed to hold the proximal row reduced and to decrease compressive loads across the lunate (Fig. 16.53).

![[Fig.16.53.png]]
> **Fig. 16.53** A high-energy injury resulting in a lunate body fracture (arrows) and associated radial styloid fracture. Plain radiographic (A) and tomographic (B and C) evaluations demonstrating a fracture to the body and volar lip of the lunate. The lunate fracture was approached through an extended carpal tunnel incision crossing in oblique fashion across the superficial wrist crease between the wrist flexor tendons and the ulnar nerve vascular bundle. D and E, The lunate fracture was stabilized with a headless screw and the radial styloid fracture secured with a headed cannulated screw.

#### Vascular Supply

The vascular supply of the lunate has been studied extensively (Fig. 16.54). Both a palmar and a dorsal blood supply are present in 74% to 100% of bones. These studies have demonstrated a single vascular blood supply in approximately 7% of lunates. Of those lunates with a dual blood supply, 33% have a single palmar and dorsal vessel for anastomosis, 66% have a three- vessel anastomosis, and 10% have a four- vessel anastomosis. In those lunates with a single nutrient vessel, interruption may lead to necrosis of the entire bone; similarly, a coronal fracture in these lunates can lead to avascularity of the opposite pole.

![[Fig.16.54.png]]
> **Fig. 16.54** The vascular supply of the lunate has been thoroughly studied, and three major vascular patterns have been identified. These have been previously described as the Y, X, and I patterns. The I pattern is the only one with a single vessel to the lunate and is considered to be most at risk for development of avascular necrosis.

Injection studies of the anterior interosseous vascular lunate have demonstrated a consistent palmar blood supply but frequently an inconsistent dorsal blood supply. On the palmar aspect, the radial, ulnar, and palmar branches of the anterior interosseous artery combine to form three transverse arches to supply the lunate. Dorsally, the radial, ulnar, and dorsal branches of the anterior interosseous artery combine to form three arches. The dorsal blood supply to the lunate is drawn from the proximal two transverse arches over the radiocarpal and intercarpal joints.

Because of internal anastomoses, both dorsal and palmar flow must be interrupted for the lunate to lose circulation. Fractures that split the lunate into dorsal and palmar halves or dislocations that leave a dorsal hinge should not be expected to result in AVN. However, 20% of lunates have only a palmar nutrient artery supply in the bone. Displaced volar fragments left untreated may place predisposed lunates at risk for AVN. These fractures may be anatomically reduced and fixed.

### Osteonecrosis

#### Kienböck Disease

The following section on Kienböck disease is edited from *Green’s Operative Hand Surgery,* seventh edition. The updates are by Gregory I. Bain, Simon BM MacLean, and David Lichtman. The author is grateful for their perspective and expertise on this topic. The standalone chapter can be found at ExpertConsult.com.

**Historical perspective.** Robert Kienböck, an Austrian radiologist, became prominent as an innovator in his field. He published his first three papers on radiodiagnostics in 1898, and in 1910, published the first seminal paper on AVN of the lunate. In this he described the pattern of lunatomalacia with porosis, sclerosis, and initial fragmentation of the proximal pole. It was initially thought to be due to repetitive trauma related to workplace activities. This was later supported by Müller in 1920, who described it as “occupational lunatomalacia.”

**Etiology and pathogenesis.** Kienböck disease typically presents in male patients in the age group of 20 to 40 years. The etiology of Kienböck disease remains controversial, but there is thought to be an “at- risk” patient who is predisposed due to anatomic factors, including the osseous morphology of the wrist and vascularity of the lunate. Mechanical factors are thought to play a part, as it is known to be more common in males, in the dominant limb, and in workers. Other factors may be important in individual cases. There have been reports of Kienböck disease in families and associated with arterial diseases such as endarteritis, thrombotic disorders such as sickle cell anemia, divers’ decompression disease from nitrogen bubbles within the lunate, cerebral palsy, and steroid medications.

```
CRITICAL POINTS: Fractures of the Lunate

Incidence
- 1% Controversy exists regarding relative frequency because Kienböck disease and congenital bipartite lunates confound diagnosis

Classiﬁcation
- Type I Palmar pole fracture Type II Osteochondral chip Type III Dorsal pole fracture Type IV Sagittal body fracture Type V Transverse body fracture Type VI Comminuted fracture

Blood Supply
- Most lunates have palmar and dorsal blood supply; 20% have palmar supply only

Mechanism
- Usually, axial compression from capitate being driven into lunate with wrist in dorsiﬂexion and ulnar deviation; slight differences in position may lead to different fracture patterns. Type I Hyperextension, possible ligamentous avulsion fracture Type II Shear Type III Shear from capitate or ligamentous (scapholunate) avulsion fracture Type IV Shear during radiocarpal fracture- dislocation Type V Hyperextension with capitate forcing dorsal half of lunate into extension and short radiolunate ligament forcing palmar half into ﬂexion; fracture in mid section results. Shear with palmar displacement of capitate in palmar perilunate injury Type VI Axial compression between radius and capitate

Special Radiographs
- CT scanning still often necessary Oblique wrist views

Treatment
- The ﬁrst step is to determine whether the pathologic process is Kienböck dis ease, congenital bipartite lunate, or acute traumatic fracture. If fracture fragments are too small but there is concern about instability, Kirschner wire to adjacent carpal bones may be needed. Carefully assess scapholunate and lunotriquetral ligaments for injury, and eval uate for carpal instability if small avulsion fractures appear benign on radio graphs or CT scans because there may be extensive ligamentous disruption. Type I Nondisplaced and small, no instability: Short- arm cast for 4 to 6 weeks Large and/or displaced, or signs of carpal instability (usually VISI as lunate is unlinked from triquetrum): ORIF with headless compression screws or Kirschner wires; may need carpal reduction and Kirschner wire stabiliza tion to adjacent bones if signs of carpal instability Type II Short- arm cast for 4 to 6 weeks; arthroscopic debridement or excision if remains symptomatic Type III Nondisplaced and small, no instability: Short- arm cast for 4 to 6 weeks Large and/or displaced, or signs of carpal instability (usually DISI as lunate is unlinked from scaphoid): ORIF with headless compression screws or Kirschner wires; may need carpal reduction and Kirschner wire stabiliza tion to adjacent bones if signs of carpal instability Type IV ORIF with headless compression screws or Kirschner wires if displaced Type V ORIF with headless compression screws or Kirschner wires if displaced or carpal instability Type VI Severe comminution or chronic (without capitate/lunate fossa arthrosis): Proximal row carpectomy

Associated Injury
- Distal radius Carpus Metacarpals

Complications
- Nonunion Avascular necrosis Carpal instability Posttraumatic arthritis
```

Osseous anatomy. The lunate is regarded as the “keystone” of the wrist and is essential for the stable equilibrium necessary for normal wrist kinematics. As the intercalated segment of the intercalated proximal carpal row, only the volar and dorsal capsule and the perilunate ligaments (the scapholunate and lunotriquetral complexes) insert on it. The lunate is cartilaginous at birth, with one ossification center. Ossification is usually complete at 1.5 to 7 years in boys, and 1 to 6 years in girls. Double ossification centers and separate ossicles can exist.

Variations in lunate shape, size, position, and the number of distal articulating facets affect load transfer from the capitate to lunate and from the lunate to the distal radius. Viegas et al. described two types of lunate based on the presence (V- type II, 65% of population) or absence (V- type I, 35% of population) of a distal ulnar facet articulating with the hamate (Fig. 16.55). Type I lunates, therefore, articulate with four bones while Type II lunates articulate with five.

![[Fig.16.55.png]]
> **Fig. 16.55** Viegas type 1 and Type 2 lunates. (Redrawn and modified from Viegas SF, Wagner K, Eng RP, et al. Medial [hamate] facet of the lunate, J Hand Surg Am 15[4]:564–571, 1990. As printed in Yeo CJ, Bain GI, Perilli E. Osseous anatomy and microanatomy of the lunate. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:13–21.)

This difference in shapes affects wrist biomechanics, and Viegastype I lunates have preferential flexion through the *midcarpal* joint, while type II lunates have preferential flexion through the *radiocarpal joint.* Not surprisingly, this also seems to affect the stresses across the lunate and a higher rate of coronal fractures and Kienböck disease are both seen in type I lunates.

Zapico also categorized the lunate into three types based on osseous differences. The Zapico type 1 lunate is trapezoidal and has intraosseous trabecular angulation exceeding 135 degrees. Associated with a *negative* ulnar variance, Zapico reported that his type 1 lunate was more susceptible to Kienböck disease since the trabecular geometry creates a potential plane of weakness (Fig. 16.56).

![[Fig.16.56.png]]
> **Fig. 16.56** Zapico lunate types. (Redrawn and modified from Geissler WB, Slade JF. Fractures of the carpal bones. In: Wolfe SW, Hotchkiss RN, Pederson WC, Kozin SH [eds]. Green’s Operative Hand Surgery, 6th ed, Philadelphia, Elsevier, 2011: 639–707. As printed in Yeo CJ, Bain GI, Perilli E. Osseous anatomy and microanatomy of the lunate. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:13–21.)

Other aspects of wrist bone morphology affect the biomechanics of the radiocarpal joint and force dissipation through the lunate. For example, a flatter radial inclination may predispose to Kienböck disease. Negative ulnar variance concentrates loading through the radial aspect of the lunate, though the relationship between ulnar variance and Kienböck disease is controversial. Hultén noted that 78% of Kienböck patients were ulnar- negative, although this observation was not confirmed by Nakamura et al. or D’Hoore et al. A negative ulnar variance is more common in the younger Kienböck patient.

Micro- CT demonstrates that the proximal subchondral bone plate of the lunate is only a single layer thick (Fig. 16.57), which may make this part of the lunate more vulnerable. Indeed, in advanced Kienböck disease this proximal surface is often fragmented while the distal surface only has a coronal fracture (Fig. 16.58).

![[Fig.16.57.png]]
> **Fig. 16.57** Microanatomy of the lunate. Three-dimensional micro-CT scan of the lunate demonstrating the thin proximal single layer of subchondral bone plate, which was measured to be 0.1 mm thick. There are spanning trabeculae principally on the radial aspect. (Modified from Low SC, Bain GI, Findlay DM, et al. External and internal bone microarchitecture in normal and Kienböck’s lunates: a whole-bone microcomputed tomography study, J Orthop Res 32[6]:826–833, 2014. As printed in Bain GI, Irisarri C. The etiology of Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:65–88. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

![[Fig.16.58.png]]
> **Fig. 16.58** Lunate fragmentation. Micro-CT sagittal images of Kienböck lunate. A, Fragmentation and collapse of the lunate. B, Reformatted image of the same lunate, superimposed on a normal lunate. Note that most of the cortex is still present but fragmented. However, there is an extensive void due to resorption of much of the medullary lunate. (Modified from Low SC, Bain GI, Findlay DM, et al. External and internal bone microarchitecture in normal and Kienböck’s lunates: a whole-bone microcomputed tomography study, J Orthop Res 32[6]:826–833, 2014. As printed in Yeo CJ, Bain GI, Morse L. Pathoanatomy of Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:53–63. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Vascular anatomy. Vascular entry points are limited to the volar and dorsal aspects of the cartilage-coated lunate. There is a network of five named vessels (radial, ulnar, anterior interosseous, deep palmar, and dorsal arteries) that divides into three volar and three smaller dorsal transverse arches. The lunate receives most of its blood supply from the palmar aspect, through the radiocarpal and intercarpal arches. Supply through the dorsal arches is less consistent.

The intraosseous vascularity can derive from volar and dorsal vessels (Fig. 16.59). Some lunates have only a single arterial supply, which may predispose to Kienböck disease. The subarticular venous plexus runs parallel to the subchondral bone plate and drains to the dorsal and palmar venous systems (Fig. 16.60).

![[Fig.16.59.png]]
> **Fig. 16.59** The intraosseous volar and arterial supply of the lunate. Note the large single volar artery that supplies the entire lunate in this case. (Modified from Lee MLH. The intraosseus arterial pattern of the carpal lunate bone and its relation to avascular necrosis, Acta Orthop Scand 33:43–55, 1963.)

![[Fig.16.60.png]]
> **Fig. 16.60** Cartilage bone interface illustrating subarticular venous plexus.

Pathology of avascular necrosis of bone. A localized compartment syndrome can develop if high- stress and repetitive loading causes intraosseous pressure to exceed systolic blood pressure (Fig. 16.61). Subsequent venous occlusion in the subarticular plexus can lead to interstitial edema and marrow fat swelling. These fat cells lyse and are phagocytosed by macrophages to form foam cells. This process can tamponade capillaries and sinusoids in the lunate. Ensuing stasis and intravascular thrombosis further accentuate ischemia leading to cellular necrosis. A repair response then causes new bone to be laid on dead trabeculae, appearing as a thickened microstructure on micro- CT. Fracture and fragmentation of the lunate occurs during this remodeling process. Within the lunates there are voids within the necrotic bone, reparative tissue, scarred fibrous tissue, and new bone formation. As the disease progresses, articular cartilage becomes fissured, fractured, and degenerated. Kissing lesions on the opposite articular surface may occur.

![[Fig.16.61.png]]
> **Fig. 16.61** Compartment syndrome of bone. Diagrammatic representation of the normal vascularization of the lunate (superior half) with the normal fat cells and venous drainage. With ischemia (inferior half), there is interstitial edema and the marrow fat cells become swollen. This leads to tamponade of the sinusoids, thus decreasing the venous outflow. This further increases the intraosseous pressure, reducing arterial inflow and produces necrosis. (Redrawn and modified from Bain GI, Irisarri C. The etiology of Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:65–88. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Hyperemia and edema may represent the first physiologic response to the stress. Should the insult persist, necrosis will occur with concomitant neovascularization of the lunate. Though at this stage the process is still reversible, the lunate becomes progressively compromised and eventually suffers structural failure. The wrist ultimately loses mechanical equilibrium as the “keystone” fails, and with load distribution compromised, there is sequential central column failure and flexion of the proximal row.

Once the lunate collapses. *Central column deformation, collapse and proximal row instability.* Once the lunate suffers coronal fracture or comminution, the capitate migrates proximally. The scapholunate and lunotriquetral ligaments strain or tear as the lunate collapses and tilts. With loss of central column height, the scaphoid flexes and the radial column shortens as well. The lunate may flex or extend depending on the extent of osseous disruption and direction of force through the lunate.

*Degenerative changes at the radiocarpal joint.* Fractures of the lunate subchondral bone plate produce irregularity of the lunate surface. Degenerative changes often involve the radial side of the wrist with excessive scaphoid flexion leading to incongruence at the radioscaphoid articulation and subsequent degenerative changes across the scaphoid fossa.

Degenerative changes at the midcarpal joint and “KDAC.”As the degenerative process progresses, kissing lesions occur between the capitate and lunate and articular changes progress with further fragmentation of the lunate. Once there is significant involvement of both carpal rows and two or more columns, Kienböck disease advanced collapse (KDAC) has occurred and a salvage procedure is now required.

Risk factors. Potential risk factors such as patient activity and lunate morphology associated with the development of Kienböck disease can be best appreciated through correlation with the known disease pathophysiology (Fig. 16.62).

![[Fig.16.62.png]]
> **Fig. 16.62** Morphologic factors associated with Kienböck disease. A, The “at-risk” factors increase the load and shear stresses on the radial aspect of the lunate. B, The “low-risk” factors are associated with a wide distribution of the stresses on the lunate. (Redrawn and modified from Bain GI, MacLean SBM, Yeo CJ, et al. The etiology and pathogenesis of Kienböck disease, J Wrist Surg 5[4]:248–254, 2016. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

The “at- risk lunate.” In patients with ulnar negative variance and in Viegas type I lunates, stress is concentrated onto the radial aspect of the lunate either due to lunate overhang at the ulnar edge of the radius or due to radial shift of the capitate load.

*The “at- risk area of the lunate.”* Lunate translation is restricted by the radiolunate ligaments acting as checkreins so that the site of impaction of the radius is always in the same location. Associated stress fractures occur at the interface between the stressed and unstressed sections, that is, the ulnar edge of the radius(Fig. 16.63).

![[Fig.16.63.png]]
> **Fig. 16.63** The uncovered type I lunate. The capitate is positioned on the radial edge of the lunate. With power grip in ulnar deviation the radial edge of the lunate is compressed onto the ulnar edge of the radius. The radiolunate ligaments prevent the lunate translating so the impaction point on the lunate remains constant. (Redrawn and modified from Bain GI, MacLean SBM, Yeo CJ, et al. The etiology and pathogenesis of Kienböck disease, J Wrist Surg 5[4]:248–254, 2016. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

*The “at- risk activity for the at- risk lunate.”* Power grip ulnarly deviates the wrist positioning the capitate to act as a nutcracker on the lunate held in position by the volar radiolunate ligaments and the radius.

A physiologic response in the lunate, apparent as osteoblastic activity on single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) scan and hyperemia on gadolinium- enhanced MRI scan, can be seen with repetitive loading. This “stress fracture in progression” is initially reversible, but, if unchecked, will progress to true trabecular failure. Microfracture of the proximal subchondral bone plate results in a “diepunch” pattern though the lunate. There can also be a split in the coronal plane when the lunate is compressed between the capitate and the underlying radius (Fig. 16.64).

![[Fig.16.64.png]]
> **Fig. 16.64** Capitate nutcracker. A, The capitate is seen at the center of the “target sign” on the radial aspect of the lunate. B, A coronal fracture propagating across the lunate. (From Bain GI, MacLean SBM, Yeo CJ, et al. The etiology and pathogenesis of Kienböck disease, J Wrist Surg 5[4]:248–254, 2016. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

**Clinical assessment.** Typically, Kienböck disease affects patients aged between 20 and 40 years. The male- to- female ratio at presentation is 2:1. Early in the disease process, Lichtman 0 to IIIA (Box 16.2 and Fig. 16.65) patients may present with intermittent dorsal wrist pain that becomes worse with stressful wrist activities, particularly involving extension. This progresses to localized swelling, tenderness, and pain compatible with a perilunate synovitis. Wrist range of movement and grip strength decrease in proportion to the pain and swelling, though forearm rotation is preserved. However, the clinical presentation can be quite variable so that some patients do not seek treatment and often pain is not severe enough to warrant surgery.

![[Fig.16.65.png]]
> **Fig. 16.65** Modified Lichtman radiologic classification. I, Normal plain radiographs, except possible linear of compression fracture; II, lunate density changes, lytic and/or sclerotic, but size, shape, anatomic relationship of carpal bones not altered; IIIA, lunate collapse, but carpal alignment maintained; IIIB, lunate collapse and carpal malalignment with fixed scaphoid flexion; IIIC, above plus coronal lunate fracture; IV, radiocarpal and/ or midcarpal arthritis. Clinical course may differ for very young and older patients. Though pediatric patients presenting in the early stages of the disease process may be similar to their adult counterparts, progressive carpal collapse is less frequently observed. Infantile and adolescent cases tend to revascularize with immobilization. (Redrawn and modified from Lichtman DM, Pientka WF 2nd, Bain GI. Kienböck disease: a new algorithm for the 21st century, J Wrist Surg 6[1]:2–10, 2017.)

**BOX 16.2 New Algorithm for the Treatment of Kienböck Disease**

**A. Patient’s Age?**

*A1. <15 years   A2. 16–20 years   A3. >70 years*

*Good natural history: Extended nonoperative management*

**B. State of the Lunate?**

*B1. Lunate intact (Lunate intact: Lichtman 0, I, II, Bain 0)*

*Protect lunate: Lunate unloading procedure*

*B2. Lunate compromised (Localized lunate disease: Lichtman IIIA, Bain 1)*

*Protect lunate and reconstruction: Unloading, VBG, Osteochondral graft*

*B3. Lunate not reconstructible (Advanced lunate disease: Lichtman IIIC, Bain 2b)*

*Motion preserving procedure: PRC, SC fusion*

**C. State of the Wrist?**

*C1—Carpal instability: Scaphoid flexion: RSA > 60 degrees (Lichtman IIIB)*

*C2—Localized degeneration (Lichtman IIIA, Bain 2a)*

*Motion preserving procedure: PRC, SC fusion (RSL fusion)*

*C3—Advanced degeneration (Lichtman IV, Bain 4)*

*Salvage: Wrist fusion*

Once the disease progresses to carpal instability (Lichtman IIIB- IV; see Fig. 16.65), the clinical picture may reflect secondary mechanical dysfunction and degenerative changes. Moderate residual stiffness with occasional flares of pain often predominate. In KDAC, radiographic and clinical features resemble that of SNAC and SLAC wrists. Wrist range of movement, strength, and functional scores have been shown to decrease with increasing disease stage.

The Lichtman classification is well accepted and brings together the radiologic osseous factors (see Fig. 16.65).

![[Fig.16.65.png]]
> **Fig. 16.65** Modified Lichtman radiologic classification. I, Normal plain radiographs, except possible linear of compression fracture; II, lunate density changes, lytic and/or sclerotic, but size, shape, anatomic relationship of carpal bones not altered; IIIA, lunate collapse, but carpal alignment maintained; IIIB, lunate collapse and carpal malalignment with fixed scaphoid flexion; IIIC, above plus coronal lunate fracture; IV, radiocarpal and/ or midcarpal arthritis. Clinical course may differ for very young and older patients. Though pediatric patients presenting in the early stages of the disease process may be similar to their adult counterparts, progressive carpal collapse is less frequently observed. Infantile and adolescent cases tend to revascularize with immobilization. (Redrawn and modified from Lichtman DM, Pientka WF 2nd, Bain GI. Kienböck disease: a new algorithm for the 21st century, J Wrist Surg 6[1]:2–10, 2017.)

In older patients, Kienböck disease occurs more commonly in women and may be associated with systemic/inflammatory disorders. The radiographs are usually more advanced though symptoms tend to be milder.

Diagnostic imaging. Clinical features of Kienböck disease are nonspecific and can resemble other wrist pathologies. Imaging allows diagnosis, staging, and exclusion of other disease processes.

Plain radiographs. Initial evaluation should include plain radiographs taken in three views: lateral, 45- degree oblique, and PA projections. The PA view should be taken in the “neutral” position with the shoulder abducted to 90 degrees, elbow flexed at 90 degrees, and forearm in neutral rotation to assess ulnar variance. Anatomic features to note include lunate size, radial inclination, and radioscaphoid angle (normal 30 to 60 degrees), though these may be difficult to measure after lunate fracture and collapse. With lunate collapse, the radiographs may demonstrate ulnar translocation or ulnar styloid carpal impingement.

Computed tomography. Plain radiographs alone often underestimate the extent of the disease process compared with CT scanning (Fig. 16.66). CT aids in assessing osteosclerosis, fracture, fragmentation, collapse, and perilunate arthritis and should be considered an essential assessment tool for all patients with Kienböck disease.

![[Fig.16.66.png]]
> **Fig. 16.66** Sagittal and coronal views on a CT scan clearly showing a coronal fracture and fragmentation of the lunate. (From MacLean SBM, Bain GI. Hand and wrist avascular necrosis. In: Weiss APC [ed]. The Textbook of Hand & Upper Extremity Surgery, 2nd ed, Chicago, American Society for Surgery of the Hand, 2020. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Magnetic resonance imaging. Abnormal marrow signal demonstrated on MRI is an early disease marker, and revascularization of the lunate restores normal marrow signal. Cartilage integrity and subchondral collapse can also be assessed.

The pathoanatomical phases identified with unenhanced MRI scan are edema, cellular necrosis, and bone remodeling. In reality, however, unenhanced MRI is limited:
- High signal in T2-weighted images does *not* differentiate between inter cellular (reversible) and intracellular (cytotoxic) edema.

- The necrotic zones cannot be reliably discerned from the neigh

boring bed.

- The *repair* zone cannot be assessed.

Recommended technique includes using a 3.0T MRI with the following sequences: proton density (PD) weighted fast spin echo (FSE) with fat- saturation (coronal and axial planes), T1- weighted FSE unenhanced coronal images, T1- weighted FSE fat- saturation after IV gadolinium contract (coronal and sagittal).

MRI with gadolinium enhancement. The perfusion state of the bone marrow can be better assessed with gadolinium- enhanced MRI on fat- saturated T1- weighted sequences (contrast- enhanced MRI [ceMRI]). Areas of enhancement are characteristic of the repair zone in Kienböck disease and normal unaffected bone marrow remains unenhanced.

The Schmitt classification outlines three patterns suggestive of a viable lunate based on ceMRI (Fig. 16.67):

![[Fig.16.67.png]]
> **Fig. 16.67** The Schmitt classification for Kienböck disease using contrast-enhanced MRI. (Redrawn from Schmitt RR, Kalb K. Advanced imaging of Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016: 121–146. Courtesy of Prof. RR Schmitt.)

- a: Normal: Homogenous enhancement of entire lunate

- b: Ischemic: Homogenous proximal enhancement, indicating per

fusion (Fig. 16.68)

![[Fig.16.68.png]]
> **Fig. 16.68** Contrast-enhanced MRI showing increased signal in the lunate. (From Bain GI, MacLean SBM, Yeo CJ, et al. The etiology and pathogenesis of Kienböck disease, J Wrist Surg 5[4]:248–254, 2016. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

- c: Partially necrotic: Heterogenous contrast with three different per

fusion zones

- Proximal: Decreased signal with no T1 enhancement indicates

osteonecrosis.
- Middle: Hyper-enhancement indicates repair
- Distal: normal signal in normal marrow fusion

Other imaging. New advanced imaging techniques, such as fourdimensional CT and SPECT, may prove to have a greater place in the assessment of complex cases.

- d: Necrotic (viability lost): Generalized signal loss, absence of per

Arthroscopic assessment. Arthroscopy allows a detailed inspection and palpation of the articular and ligamentous structures of the joint with minimal risk of soft tissue disruption.

Diagnostic arthroscopy is performed with traction and a 2.7- mm, 30- degree arthroscope. Routine portals (3- 4, 6R, midcarpal radial [MCR], and midcarpal ulnar [MCU]) are used. Since fluid pressure can collapse the capillaries, dry arthroscopy provides superior visualization of articular surfaces and synovitis. Inspection and probing of the articular surfaces of the lunate and surrounding carpus need to be performed.

Key arthroscopic findings include the following:

1. “Functional” articular surfaces appear smooth and glistening and firm to palpation. Minor fibrillation is still considered a functional articulation.

2. “Nonfunctional” articular surfaces include extensive fibril lation, fissuring, floating chondral surface, or fracture(Fig. 16.69).
3. Synovitis is identified in all patients with Kienböck disease, and the extent of the synovitis is related to the severity of the condition.

![[Fig.16.69.png]]
> **Fig. 16.69** The Bain and Begg arthroscopic classification for the treatment of Kienböck disease. (Redrawn from Bain GI, Durrant A. An articular-based approach to Kienböck’s avascular necrosis of the lunate, Tech Hand Up Extrem Surg15[1]:41–47, 2011.)

4. “Concealed fracture” of the lunate may be identified with prob ing. A thin line or crease in the opalescent articular surface (similar to that seen in a proximal pole fracture of the scaphoid) is a telltale sign.

5. “Floating” proximal lunate surface is when the collapsed lunate is distracted during traction but collapses with probing. It is an important finding and indicates that reconstruction of the proximal lunate needs to be considered.
6. Scapholunate and lunotriquetral ligaments are often torn adja cent to the lunate fracture and collapsed.
7. Ulnar styloid impingement is associated with collapse of the lunate and ulnar translocation.

Based on the preoperative imaging and the arthroscopic findings, a definitive plan can now be developed. The Bain and Begg classification and algorithm utilizes the arthroscopic findings to direct treatment (see Fig. 16.69). The grade identified is based on the number of articulations involved in the central column, ranging from 0 to 4. There can be involvement of the lunate facet and proximal lunate (2a) (Fig. 16.70) or a coronal fracture that includes the proximal and distal aspects of the lunate (2b). By defining the functional articulations, their location, and the extent of wrist involvement, the algorithm directs surgical management toward the optimal motion preserving procedure. The nonfunctional articular surfaces are managed with excision, fusion, or bypass, preserving functional articulations to maximize final wrist mobility.

![[Fig.16.70.png]]
> **Fig. 16.70** Arthroscopic image of a nonfunctional proximal lunate articular surface, with sclerosis and ulceration of the proximal articular surface. (From Bain G, MacLean S, Tse WL, et al. Kienböck disease and arthroscopy: assessment, classification, and treatment, J Wrist Surg 5[4]:255–260, 2016. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

![[Fig.16.69.png]]
> **Fig. 16.69** The Bain and Begg arthroscopic classification for the treatment of Kienböck disease. (Redrawn from Bain GI, Durrant A. An articular-based approach to Kienböck’s avascular necrosis of the lunate, Tech Hand Up Extrem Surg15[1]:41–47, 2011.)

**Classifications and new algorithm for treatment.** There are three main classifications used for Kienböck disease. The Lichtman osseous classification assesses the osseous state of the lunate and wrist. Although initially described utilizing plain radiographs, it was later modified to include MRI assessment. The Schmitt classification assesses vascular perfusion in the lunate based on contrast- enhanced MRI. The Bain and Begg classification assesses the articular surfaces of the lunate and wrist during wrist arthroscopy.

The new algorithm developed by Lichtman and Bain incorporates the osseous, chondral, and vascular status of the lunate as well as the secondary effects on the wrist and combines elements of the three main classification systems (see Box 16.2 and Fig. 16.71).

![[Fig.16.71.png]]
> **Fig. 16.71** New algorithm for treatment, which uses the primary factors to determine the treatment recommendation. The questions (green), classification (yellow), treatment principles (gray), and treatment (blue). PRC, Proximal row carpectomy; SC, scaphocapitate; VBG, vascular bone graft. (Copyright © Dr Gregory Bain.)

**Management.** The choice of management of Kienböck disease has been controversial since Robert Kienböck first described the condition. The natural history remains poorly understood, but we do know that the disease occurs along a spectrum from minimal changes to advanced collapse and degeneration. The associated pain may range from minor to debilitating. Therefore management should be based on specific treatment aims.

Aims of treatment. The aims of treatment are to:

1. Improve the patient’s pain.
2. Protect the lunate from collapse.
3. Protect the wrist joint from collapse and degeneration.
4. If the lunate is compromised, consider reconstruction.
5. If the lunate is not reconstructible or the wrist is unstable or degenerate, consider a motion- preserving reconstructive procedure.
6. If the wrist is not reconstructible, consider a salvage procedure.

Factors for consideration. There are many factors to consider in individualizing the management plan including patient characteristics and the state of the lunate and wrist (Table 16.6).

**TABLE 16.6 Factors to Consider in Planning Management**

| | Factors–2 | Factors–3 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Patient | Demographics | Age, sex, occupation, activities, comorbidities, medications, smoking |
| | History | Injury, pain, aggravating factors, duration of symptoms |
| | Examination | Swelling, tenderness, range of motion, grip strength |
| Lunate | Loading | Lunate type, lunate translation, ulnar variance, radial inclination |
| | Osseous | Sclerosis, fragmentation, coronal fracture, collapse |
| | Articular | Functional articulation, lamination, degeneration |
| | Vascularity | Vascular pattern, ischemia, necrosis, revascularization |
| Wrist | Joint | Synovitis, contracture |
| | Stability | Carpal collapse (RSA >60); scapholunate and lunotriquetral tears |
| | Degeneration | Localized, generalized |

Age. Age is an important prognostic factor in Kienböck disease, and the vast majority of pediatric Kienböck disease patients will experience complete radiographic and clinical improvement following a period of immobilization in a splint or cast. In the older child or teenager with “juvenile osteomalacia,” recalcitrant cases do occur. Patients with ulnar- negative variance may benefit from a radial shortening osteotomy. Ulnar- neutral or ulnar- negative patients may benefit from radial closing wedge osteotomy or temporary STT joint pinning.

Intact lunate. For the intact lunate (B1), the lunate can be protected with a lunate unloading procedure.

Reconstructible lunate. The reconstructible lunate (B2) is usually compromised with collapse of the proximal osseous lunate, but with intact articular cartilage. In this case a bone graft can be used to help fill the void in the proximal lunate. A lunate unloading procedure may also be included to protect the compromised lunate.

A scaphocapitate wire will reduce loading of the lunate to facilitate healing, especially for the type B2 lunate.

Unreconstructible lunate. With severe fragmentation and collapse (B3), the unsalvageable lunate requires excision. If there is a fixed secondary carpal instability (C1) or degenerative changes in the central column (C2), then a motion- preserving procedure should be considered.

What can the surgeon offer in the health care system?. This is an important question, with each surgeon and health system having different challenges. There are many variables including the surgeon’s experience and skills, hospital facilities, cost of health care, insurance status, and government restrictions (e.g., U.S. Food and Drug Administration [FDA]). These factors in part explain the considerable variability in recommended treatment.

Advanced techniques. The above concepts are aimed to provide care for the majority of patients with Kienböck disease. There are some patients and some techniques that require advanced skills or resources. Some of these techniques are still being developed and may become more widespread in the future. The three categories of surgery where this specifically applies are advanced arthroscopic reconstructive surgery, free microsurgical tissue transfers, and implant arthroplasty.

**Treatment.** A trial of nonoperative treatment should include activity modification, simple analgesics, and a wrist splint for 3 months. Other options than can be considered include nonsteroidal antiinflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), cortisone injections, and cast immobilization. Those patients who fail nonoperative modalities may be considered for surgery.

Natural history of nonoperative treatment. Stahl recommended prolonged immobilization for Kienböck disease. Tajima reviewed 80 patients with Kienböck disease with a follow- up of 42 years and found no appreciable difference in the end results of nonoperative or surgical treatment.

Kristensen and colleagues followed 46 patients with Kienböck disease treated nonoperatively for a minimum of 5 years, with a mean of 20 years. Although 82% of the patients had arthritic changes of the wrist at follow- up, only 25% had significant pain. Taniguchi and colleagues found that although radiographic findings worsened in 70% of 20 patients followed for 35 years, only 20% had significant symptoms limiting their activities. However, a study by Keith and colleagues reviewed 33 patients treated nonoperatively for Kienböck disease. They found a predictable pattern of deterioration of motion, grip strength, and DASH scores. In a study by Viljakka and colleagues, long- term (7 to 26 years) natural history of Lichtman stage III Kienböck showed that outcomes were highly variable with some patients improving, some remaining the same, and some deteriorating. They conclude that observation is an option.

Basic arthroscopy. Diagnostic arthroscopy is performed as described in the assessment section. All patients with Kienböck disease will have varying degrees of synovitis.

Synovectomy and joint debridement can be performed and should include removal of loose bodies and debridement of torn and unstable surfaces with a full radius shaver and/or pituitary rongeurs (Fig. 16.72).

![[Fig.16.72.png]]
> **Fig. 16.72** Arthroscopic removal of necrotic bone from the proximal lunate using pituitary rongeurs. (From Bain G, MacLean S, Tse WL, et al. Kienböck disease and arthroscopy: assessment, classification, and treatment, J Wrist Surg 5[4]:255–260, 2016. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Principles of advanced reconstructive arthroscopic procedures.Carpal bone excision is commenced with a small bur and then a large shoulder bur to improve efficiency. Releasing the ligament attachments allows the bone to be more easily removed with a pituitary rongeur.

Fusion surfaces are prepared by debriding the articular cartilage to bleeding subchondral bone. Start on the gravity- dependent areas so that bleeding is less likely to obstruct visibility. Irrigation reduces heat generation which may lead to nonunion.

Carpal alignment can be achieved (with Kirschner wire joystick) under fluoroscopic imaging.

Fixation for a limited wrist fusion can be achieved percutaneously with Kirschner wires and cannulated screws.

Cancellous bone graft can be added via a trochar or a small syringe with the tip removed. A Foley catheter can be inserted to prevent bone graft from extending into the joint space.

Unloading procedures. The unloading procedures are designed to decrease the recurrent compressing forces on the vulnerable lunate. The preferred choice of unloading procedure depends on wrist morphology. A radial shortening osteotomy is appropriate with ulnar negative variance while capitate shortening is recommended with neutral or positive ulnar variance. An STT or scaphocapitate fusion and/or Kirschner wire fixation will also unload the lunate (Kirschner wire is only temporary).

Radial shortening osteotomy. Early studies observed a greater preponderance of ulnar minus variance in Kienböck patients versus controls. This has not subsequently been supported in the literature.Horii et al. found that joint leveling reduces forces across the radiolunate joint by 45%.

The exact amount of resection is debated. Trumble and colleagues suggested that 2 mm of resection decompresses through the compromised lunate but does not cause ulnocarpal impingement or distal radioulnar joint (DRUJ) incongruity. Nakamura and colleagues showed that shortening greater than 4 mm leads to poor outcomes.Ulnar lengthening has been proposed as an alternative, but enthusiasm for this approach is limited by nonunion risk and autograft harvest morbidity. Multiple studies have shown functional but not necessarily radiographic improvement following radial shortening.

Variations of this theme include epiphysiodesis in the growing child and radial lateral closing wedge osteotomy in the ulnar- positive patient (especially if there is increased radial inclination). The relationship between radial inclination and Kienböck disease is debated. Watanabe reported decreased radiolunate and ulnolunate joint forces following closing wedge osteotomy. More recent biomechanical data has been inconsistent.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: RADIAL SHORTENING OSTEOTOMY

A volar FCR approach is performed and the pronator quadratus (PQ) released. The osteotomy is performed at the metaphyseal/diaphyseal junction, with the aim to create a neutral ulnar variance. The osteotomy is stabilized with a volar locking distal radius plate (Fig. 16.73).

![[Fig.16.73.png]]
> **Fig. 16.73** Radial shortening osteotomy. A, The procedure is performed through an FCR bed approach, and the plate is provisionally fixed distally and proximally though the oblong hole. B, The plate is removed and an osteotomy performed with a narrow blade with irrigation. The proximal osteotomy is performed first through ¾ of the radius only. The second, more distal osteotomy is completed before completing the proximal osteotomy. C, The osteotomy is compressed with a bone holding clamp placed between the screw in the oblong hole and a distal part of the plate. The remainder of the screws are inserted to stabilize the construct and (D) final radiographs obtained. K wires, Kirschner wires. (Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

A removable wrist brace is applied and active movements encouraged. At 3 months, if radiographs demonstrate union, the patient can resume full activities.

Capitate shortening. In 1986 Almquist described capitate shortening and reported decreased radiolunate loading (66%) compared with radial shortening (45%). Cortical disruption in the capitate may increase local circulation to the lunate and this technique can be used in patients with early lunate fragmentation and ulnar negative variance.

The capitate head has several vascular penetrations through the volar capsule along the articular margin so the proximal pole of the capitate has a retrograde arterial supply similar to the scaphoid.

For outcomes of capitate shortening, Almquist reported an 83% satisfaction rate and grip strength of the affected hand to be 80% of the opposite side.

Arimitsu and colleagues reported on 22 of 23 good to excellent results for partial capitate shortening (shortening only the part of the capitate that articulates with the lunate) in stage III disease (14 IIIA, 4 IIIB, 5 IIIC) with median follow- up of 48 months. Pain, motion, and grip strength significantly improved ( *P* < 0.01) from preoperative to postoperative.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: CAPITATE SHORTENING

Via a dorsal approach, the capitate and lunate are exposed (Fig. 16.74). A single transverse cut with an oscillating saw is made at the distal capitate waist, with care taken to not disrupt the volar soft tissue envelope.

![[Fig.16.74.png]]
> **Fig. 16.74** Capitate shortening osteotomy. A, Via a dorsal approach, a single cut dorsal waist osteotomy is performed. B, A small segment of the base of the third metacarpal is excised to allow the Kirschner wire to be advanced. A guide wire is introduced under fluoroscopic control. C, Single cannulated screw provides compression. (From Bain GI, Krishna SV, MacLean SBM, et al. Single cut, single screw capitate shortening osteotomy for Kienböck’s disease, J Wrist Surg 9[4]:276–282, 2020. Copyright © Gregory Bain, MD.)

Compression and fixation are achieved with a cannulated headless compression. We prefer using a retrograde approach (distal to proximal). Excision of the proximal dorsal base of the third metacarpal allows correct screw placement. A guide wire is then advanced just volar to the dorsal cortex of the capitate, into the proximal head. A cannulated screw is then advanced over the wire (Fig. 16.75).

![[Fig.16.75.png]]
> **Fig. 16.75** Postoperative radiographs following single cut capitate osteotomy. (From Bain GI, Krishna SV, MacLean SBM, et al. Single cut, single screw capitate shortening osteotomy for Kienböck’s disease, J Wrist Surg 9[4]:276–282, 2020. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Postoperatively a plaster slab is applied for 1 week, then a removable splint for 6 weeks. Once union has been confirmed, the patient can return to full activities.

Lunate reconstruction. Lunate reconstruction is indicated if compromised (B2), but before the lunate has become unreconstructible (B3), or the wrist has developed carpal instability (C1) or degeneration (C2 and C3). Prior to collapse and while the cartilage shell and adjacent articulations are still intact, bone grafting can be a good option to address the necrotic lunate (i.e., Lichtman Bain- B1 lunate).

Proximal osseous lunate compromise but with intact articular cartilage: This osseous reconstruction can be performed with a bone graft, or pedicle or free vascular bone graft (VBG).

Osseous *and* articular proximal lunate compromise: This osteochondral defect would ideally require an osteochondral graft. The options are a nonvascularized graft, local vascularized graft (e.g., pisiform), or free vascularized graft such as a medial femoral trochlear graft (MFTG).

Contraindications to VBG are unreconstructible lunate (coronal fracture, a nonfunctional articular surface, degeneration of adjacent articulations) or previous surgery that may have compromised the dorsal vessels (volar VBG may still be an option). Relative contraindications include tobacco abuse, peripheral vascular disease, and steroid use.

Nonvascularized bone graft. Cancellous bone graft or aspirated bone marrow can be harvested from the distal radius and packed into the lunate defect as an isolated procedure or supplemental to a vascularized graft. Nonvascularized autograft bone heals by creeping substitution, which involves revascularization, osteogenesis, and remodeling. Prolonged immobilization is required since resorption during this process significantly weakens the bone.

Vascularized pedicle flaps for Kienböck diseaseRationale of VBG. The VBG includes trabeculae, living osteocytes and osteoblasts, and an intact blood supply. The cells remain alive and contribute to the bone mass, resulting in less bone resorption, decreasing the risk of fatigue fracture and nonunion. The blood flow is maintained and increases over time. This surgery can be challenging and requires detailed understanding of the vascular anatomy, meticulous dissection with loupe magnification, and sculpturing of the graft.

Types of VBG. Many types have been described:

(a) Ligated vascular bundle (Hori) technique
(b) Dorsal capsular-based vascularized flap (Fig. 16.76)
(c) Vascular pedicle with bone graft:
- Dorsal
- 1,2 intercompartmental artery (Zaidemberg) (Fig. 16.77)
- 4,5 extracompartmental artery (Fig. 16.78)
- Volar
- Transverse volar carpal artery with cancellous bone
- Pisiform graft (bone and articular cartilage)
(d) Free vascularized graft—MFTG. This is an osteochondral graft that can replace the diseased bone and cartilage of the proximal lunate. A cutaneous paddle can be added to assist in skin coverage or as a marker of perfusion.

![[Fig.16.76.png]]
> **Fig. 16.76** Dorsal capsular based vascularized flap (A). The distal radius (R) and dorsal capsule (C) are accessed through a dorsal incision just ulnar to the Lister tubercle. The graft is outlined on the dorsal capsule and distal radius (B). Diagrammatic view of the graft. (A from and B redrawn from Sotereanos DG, Darlis NA, Dailiana ZH, et al. A capsularbased vascularized distal radius graft for proximal pole scaphoid pseudarthrosis, J Hand Surg Am 31:580–587, 2006.)

![[Fig.16.77.png]]
> **Fig. 16.77** Anatomic representation of the 1,2 intercompartmental supraretinacular artery (1,2 ICSRA) VBG. R, Radius; RA, radial artery; S, scaphoid. (Redrawn from Albinder WR, Shin AY. Vascularized pedicle flaps for Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:199–213. Used with permission from the Mayo Foundation for Medical Education and Research. All rights reserved.)

![[Fig.16.78.png]]
> **Fig. 16.78** The 4 + 5 ECA VBG. A, Elevation of a dorsal retinacular flap over the fifth compartment. EDC, Extensor digitorum communis; EDM, extensor digiti minimi. B, Representation of the 4 + 5 ECA VBG. C, Arthrotomy performed to expose lunate. ECA, Extracompartmental artery. D, Harvested 4 + 5 ECA VBG with ligation of vessel proximally to create a long pedicle with retrograde flow through the fifth ECA. E, Insetting of the 4 + 5 VBG into the lunate cavity. (Images redrawn and modified from Moran SL, Cooney WP, Berger RA, et al. The use of the 4 + 5 extensor compartmental vascularized bone graft for the treatment of Kienbock’s disease, J Hand Surg Am 30[1]:50–58, 2005. As printed in Albinder WR, Shin AY. Vascularized pedicle flaps for Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:199– 213. Used with permission from the Mayo Foundation for Medical Education and Research. All rights reserved.)

Comparative studies are lacking and there is currently no consensus as to the best option. The more popular techniques appear to be the transverse volar carpal artery, Hori vascular bundle technique,and the 4,5 extracompartmental artery.

Principles of vascularized bone graft for Kienböck disease. Expose the lunate, pedicle, and donor site. Place 21- gauge needles into the adjacent articulations to aid in orientation.

Prepare the lunate with a cortical window using a bur with irrigation. Remove the necrotic bone with a curette and/or rongeur. Measure the defect.

Graft harvest. Plan to make the graft a little oversized and then draw it with a surgical marker. Remember, a quality tradesman will “mark twice and cut once.” Usually a sharp osteotome is effective and will tend to compact the margins of the graft, making it less likely to fragment.

If the cortex is particularly hard, an osteotome can fracture the cortex or impact the underlying cancellous graft. This can be avoided by drilling the corners with a 1.6- mm Kirschner wire, creating curved corners less prone to fracture propagation. The cortex can then be cut cleanly with a narrow oscillating saw under copious irrigation to avoid thermal necrosis.

Mobilize the graft pedicle and a cuff of soft tissue to protect the delicate venae comitantes. Only use bipolar cautery on the graft or pedicle. Stretching or kinking of the graft will compromise the venous drainage and threaten the graft. A suture in the periosteum on the leading corner of the graft offers a loose handle to position the graft.

Fashion the graft with a small bone cutter and rongeur to achieve a press fit. This is tedious but is a critical investment in graft stability and clinical outcome. For a dorsal graft, flex the wrist, as this will open the cortical window. Grasping the width of the graft with a small hemostat clamp allows it to be manipulated with precision and inset so that it is captured by the recipient cortex.

Secure the graft with a small Kirschner wire, suture, or suture anchor. Add cancellous graft to fill any remaining defects in the lunate.

Unload the graft with a percutaneous scaphocapitate 1.2- or 1.6mm Kirschner wire inserted under fluoroscopy to protect the lunate from collapse during the revascularization phase.

Postoperatively, a plaster slab is applied, the arm elevated on a pillow, and finger mobilization encouraged. At 1 week, a cast is applied and advanced to a removable brace at 6 weeks. At 12 weeks, the scaphocapitate wire is removed if radiographs demonstrate union of the graft. Activities are then increased and strengthening exercises commenced. The patient can return to sport once the graft has consolidated, usually at 6 months.

Anatomy. The four arteries that supply the distal radius and ulna are the radial, ulnar, anterior interosseous, and posterior interosseous arteries (Fig. 16.79). These vessels give the following branches, which can be used for pedicle grafts: the 1,2 and 2,3 ICSRA lie superficial to the extensor retinaculum; the fourth and fifth extensor compartmental arteries (4 and 5 ECA) lie deep to the fourth and fifth compartments.The transverse volar carpal artery spans between the radial and ulnar arteries just distal to the PQ.

![[Fig.16.79.png]]
> **Fig. 16.79** Vascular anatomy of the dorsal distal radius. 1,2 ICSRA, 1,2 Intercompartmental supraretinacular artery); 2,3 ICSRA, 2,3 intercompartmental supraretinacular artery; 4th ECA, fourth extracompartmental artery; 5th ECA, fourth extracompartmental artery; aAIA, anterior branch of anterior interosseous artery; AIA, anterior interosseous artery; dICa, dorsal intercarpal arch; dRCa, dorsal radiocarpal arch; dSRa, dorsal supraretinacular arch; pAIA, posterior branch of the anterior interosseous artery; PIA, posterior interosseous artery; RA, radial artery; UA, ulnar artery. (Redrawn and modified from Shin AY, Bishop AT, Berger RA. Vascularized pediculed bone grafts for disorders of the carpus, Tech Hand Upper Extrem Surg 2[2]:94–109, 1998. As printed in Albinder WR, Shin AY. Vascularized pedicle flaps for Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:199–213. Used with permission from the Mayo Foundation for Medical Education and Research. All rights reserved.)

The 1,2 ICSRA originates from the radial artery about 48 mm proximal to the radiocarpal joint and lies over the retinaculum. The vessel travels deep to brachioradialis and courses superficial to the 1,2 intercompartmental septum of the extensor retinaculum before entering the snuffbox to anastomose with the radial artery, radiocarpal arch, or the intercarpal arch.

The 2,3 ICSRA usually originates from the anterior interosseous artery (AIA) or its posterior division. It courses over the retinaculum on the Lister tubercle and anastomoses with the dorsal intercarpal arch, the dorsal radiocarpal arch, and/or the fourth ECA. It has a larger mean internal diameter and a larger arc of rotation than the 1,2 ICSRA, which increases its suitability for the treatment of Kienböck disease.

The fourth ECA is usually supplied by the posterior division of the AIA or the fifth ECA. It lies on the radial aspect of the fourth extensor compartment, or in the 3,4 septum. It anastomoses with the dorsal intercarpal arch, dorsal radiocarpal arch, the 2,3 ICSRA and/or the fifth ECA. Sotereanos and colleagues described a capsular- based VBG utilizing this vasculature for the treatment of scaphoid nonunion as an alternative graft.

The fifth ECA has the largest diameter of the dorsal vessels and arises from the posterior division of the AIA. It is most commonly found radially in the fifth extensor compartment but can be found in the 4,5 septum. It may have additional communications with the fourth ECA, the dorsal radiocarpal arch, the 2,3 ICSRA, and/or the oblique dorsal artery of the distal ulna.

Hori graft.This is the simplest of the vascularized grafts. The concept is to insert a vessel into the lunate, which allows revascularization to occur. The second dorsal intermetacarpal artery is mobilized and ligated. This vessel is then inserted into the lunate (Fig. 16.80). Extra cancellous bone graft can be added.

![[Fig.16.80.png]]
> **Fig. 16.80** Second intermetacarpal artery vascularized graft. A, The second intermetacarpal artery is mobilized and (B) inserted and (C) stabilized into the lunate. Cancellous graft is often usually added. (Modified from Tamai S, Yajima H, Ono H. Hand Clin, 9[3]:455–466, 1993.)

Technique: fourth and fifth extracompartmental artery VBG. The fifth ECA with accompanying venae comitantes are identified. These lie near the fourth ECA in the floor of the compartment, adjacent to or within the septum between the fourth and fifth extensor compartments. A radially based flap in the extensor retinaculum is elevated from the fifth to the third compartment. The fifth ECA is traced proximally to its origin at the posterior division of the AIA. The fourth ECA is then identified, traced distally, and elevated.

Moran popularized this graft, which is centered 1.1 cm proximal to the lunate facet. The posterior division of the AIA is ligated proximal to the fourth and fifth ECA, creating a retrograde flow pedicle (see Fig. 16.78). The graft is rotated as it is advanced into the lunate.

![[Fig.16.78.png]]
> **Fig. 16.78** The 4 + 5 ECA VBG. A, Elevation of a dorsal retinacular flap over the fifth compartment. EDC, Extensor digitorum communis; EDM, extensor digiti minimi. B, Representation of the 4 + 5 ECA VBG. C, Arthrotomy performed to expose lunate. ECA, Extracompartmental artery. D, Harvested 4 + 5 ECA VBG with ligation of vessel proximally to create a long pedicle with retrograde flow through the fifth ECA. E, Insetting of the 4 + 5 VBG into the lunate cavity. (Images redrawn and modified from Moran SL, Cooney WP, Berger RA, et al. The use of the 4 + 5 extensor compartmental vascularized bone graft for the treatment of Kienbock’s disease, J Hand Surg Am 30[1]:50–58, 2005. As printed in Albinder WR, Shin AY. Vascularized pedicle flaps for Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:199– 213. Used with permission from the Mayo Foundation for Medical Education and Research. All rights reserved.)

The VBG is trimmed to size and rotated as it is inserted into the lunate—the proximal- distal length becomes the depth of the lunate, and the width of the radial cortex becomes the pillar to maintain the lunate height. Cancellous graft fills any defects and a scaphocapitate Kirschner wire unloads the lunate until graft consolidation. Moran and colleagues reported on 26 patients with Kienböck disease who had the 4,5 ECA vascularized graft. Although they had a short follow- up of 3 months, 92% of patients had significant improvement in their pain. Grip strength improved from 50% to 89% of the unaffected side. Satisfactory results were seen in 85% of patients based on the Lichtman outcome score. Of patients who had a postoperative MRI, 71% showed evidence of revascularization.

Transverse volar carpal artery VBG. Kuhlmann described a graft harvested from the volar aspect of the radius based on the transverse volar carpal artery (TVCA). Mathoulin used this VBG in conjunction with a radial shortening osteotomy (Fig. 16.81). The artery forms a “T” anastomosis with the distal branch of the AIA and a branch of the ulnar artery over the volar distal radioulnar joint. The branches of the TVCA pass deep to the PQ muscle and then penetrate the distal radial epiphysis. To safely harvest the graft, these periosteal vessels must be maintained and a strip of the distal radial periosteum included.

![[Fig.16.81.png]]
> **Fig. 16.81** Volar vascularized graft. A, Volar approach to the distal radius, and radial shortening osteotomy performed. An open carpal tunnel release is also performed to allow adequate exposure of the volar carpus. B, The transverse volar carpal artery is just distal to the pronator quadratus. C, The graft and pedicle mobilized. D, Volar cortical window created and vascularized bone added. A wire is added to stabilize the graft. E, Postoperative radiograph, with radial shortening and lunate Kirschner wire. (A, D, E, from and B and C redrawn from Mathoulin C, Wahegaonkar AL. Revascularization of the lunate by a volar vascularized bone graft and an osteotomy of the radius in treatment of the Kienböck’s disease, Microsurgery 29[5]:373–378, 2009.)

Via the distal part of the Henry approach, the radial artery is identified and the flexor tendons retracted ulnarward. If extra access is required, taking care to preserve the palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve, a step cut incision enables a carpal tunnel release to be performed.

The wrist is flexed and the flexor tendons retracted to expose the TVCA at the distal margin of the superficial fascia of PQ. The radial periosteum is incised distal to the vessel and pronator fascia is released proximal to the vessel.

The pedicle is elevated subperiosteally off the volar rim of the radius and the PQ muscle released from the distal ulnar aspect of the radius, taking care to preserve the small vessels and periosteum. DRUJ and the radiocarpal joint are identified and not inadvertently violated. A 10 mm osteotome is placed obliquely to create a pyramidal graft, which is gently elevated with a smaller osteotome. The graft and pedicle are mobilized back to the radial artery.

The volar lunate is exposed via a midline capsulotomy, with transverse “T” extension. A volar cortical window is created in the lunate and the necrotic bone is removed. The vascularized graft is fashioned to fit the lunate defect and then stabilized with a fine Kirschner wire. A loose capsular repair is performed to avoid compromising the pedicle. A scaphocapitate Kirschner wire unloads the lunate until graft consolidation.

Medial femoral trochlea graft. The medial femoral trochlear (MFT) graft, more commonly utilized for reconstruction of scaphoid nonunions, is a free vascularized osteochondral graft based on the descending geniculate artery (DGA) (Fig. 16.82). The DGA has a longitudinal and a transverse branch. The transverse branch passes anteriorly and invests in the periosteum surrounding the medial femoral trochlea, which has a similar curvature to the proximal lunate.

![[Fig.16.82.png]]
> **Fig. 16.82** Medial femoral trochea graft to reconstruct proximal lunate. Portion of MFT harvested to provide vascularized osteocartilaginous reconstruction of the proximal lunate. Note the preservation of the native cartilage effacing the midcarpal joint. Vascular anatomy demonstrates branching pattern and supply of trochlea periosteum. A, Descending geniculate artery; B, transverse branch to MFT; C, longitudinal branch; D, superomedial geniculate artery. (Redrawn from Higgins JP, Burger H. Osteochondral free flap reconstruction of advanced Kienbock’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:215–226. Copyright © The Curtis National Hand Center, 2012. Used with permission.)

*Technique.* Via a dorsal approach, the fragmented, proximal lunate is excised while the scapholunate and lunotriquetral ligaments are preserved to retain the linkage of the proximal carpal row. The radial artery in the snuffbox is prepared to receive the graft.

The graft is harvested via an incision made between the patella and the medial femoral condyle. The DGA pedicle is mobilized, leaving the periosteal vessels attached to the bone. An oscillating saw and sharp osteotomes are used to harvest the bone and articular cartilage.

The graft is fashioned to match the lunate defect and then fitted into the lunate defect so that the chondral surface of the graft creates a new lunate articulation. The graft is fixed to the native lunate using miniheadless screws or Kirschner wires.

The vascular anastomoses, usually end- to- side into the radial artery and end- to- end with the venae comitantes, are then performed in the snuffbox following tourniquet release.

Postoperatively the patient remains on the ward to keep the wrist warm (to encourage vasodilation) and for vascular monitoring. The patient can mobilize the knee without support and is discharged with daily aspirin 325 mg for 3 weeks. At 7 to 10 days, a full short- arm cast is applied and maintained for 12 weeks. A CT scan at 12 weeks is used to confirm union.

Motion- preserving procedures. Once the lunate is not reconstructible or carpal collapse or localized degeneration has occurred, a wrist reconstructive procedure is the preferred treatment. The options include a proximal row carpectomy, limited wrist fusion, and wrist arthroplasty (see Salvage section).

Proximal row carpectomy. Proximal row carpectomy (PRC) is an excision of the fragmented lunate as well as the remaining proximal carpal row so that the proximal capitate articulates with the lunate fossa. The prerequisites are functional articular surfaces and an intact RSC ligament. The clinical outcome is usually pain improvement, functional arc of 70 to 80 degrees, and grip strength of 68% of the contralateral side. Delayed development of radiocapitate arthritis has been reported in up to 87% of patients. Although it is often asymptomatic,PRC should be used cautiously in high- demand patients younger than 45 years.

We have 2- to 12- year follow- up on 12 patients treated with PRC for Bain and Begg grades 1 and 2b Kienböck disease. In this selected cohort, PRC provided excellent outcomes for pain, satisfaction, and functional scores. No patients have required additional salvage procedures. Especially if low demand or nonunion risk, PRC is our preferred procedure in this cohort.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: PROXIMAL ROW CARPECTOMY

Via a dorsal approach the proximal carpal row is identified and excised with sharp dissection. A metatarsal elevator aids safe dissection and delivery of the carpal bones. Care is taken to protect the proximal capitate, lunate fossa, radial artery, and volar ligaments. The capsule is closed with an absorbable suture (Fig. 16.83). A bulky crepe bandage is applied for 1 week, followed by a removable brace. The patient can mobilize the wrist as comfort allows. The patient should be advised that it will often take 6 to 12 months for the wrist to stabilize.

![[Fig.16.83.png]]
> **Fig. 16.83** A, CT scan showing a coronal fracture and fragmentation of the lunate. The lunate fossa and capitate head were found to be functional on arthroscopic assessment (Bain and Begg B2) and a proximal row carpectomy was performed. B, Radiograph 5 years following surgery. The patient has good wrist motion and function. (Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Limited wrist fusions. The indication for limited wrist fusions is localized disease of the carpus amendable to fusion with preservation of other functional articulations. Limited wrist fusions have been used extensively for osteoarthritis and Kienböck disease. Watson promoted the STT fusion for Kienböck disease, although more recently scaphocapitate fusion (SCF) has become more popular.

Principles of limited wrist fusion. *Surgical technique.* A *“closing fusion”* technique involves excision of the entire articular surface and compressing the opposing surfaces (Fig. 16.84). The alternative, an *“open fusion,”* maintains the height of the carpus by keeping some of the articular surface, and adding bone graft to the defect. The closing fusion technique is an intrinsically more stable and more likely to unite.

![[Fig.16.84.png]]
> **Fig. 16.84** Open versus closed fusion techniques. Open fusion uses an intercalary graft to maintain carpal height, but can compromise stability as the graft resorbs. The closing fusion provides greater apposition, with greater stability and an increased union rate but at the expense of losing carpal height. (Modified from Bain GI, McGuire DT. Decision making for partial carpal fusions, J Wrist Surg 1[2]:103–114, 2012. As printed in Ha NB, Phadnis J, Bain GI. Radioscapholunate fusion in Kienböck’s disease. In: Lichtman DM, Bain GI [eds]. Kienböck’s Disease: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment, New York, Springer, 2016:241–247. Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Nonunion risk can be reduced by avoiding thermal bone necrosis during burring, optimizing the size of the fusion mass, and adding supplemental cancellous graft from the distal radius. Fixation can be performed with plates, K wires, staples, or screws. Fluoroscopy is used to ensure that the fusion mass is aligned and internal fixation correctly positioned.
