Expectations need to be realistic since clinical improvement may take 6 months. Hand therapy assists with the swelling, motion and strength.

Postoperatively the wrist is immobilized in a plaster slab for 1 week but advanced to a removable splint if stable fixation was achieved. A short- arm cast is maintained for 6 weeks if fixation is limited to Kirschner wires or the patient deemed unreliable. Sport and heavy activities are avoided until radiographic union typically at 3 months.

Scaphocapitate fusion. An SCF redirects the load through the radial column to bypass the diseased central column. The indication is advanced disease of the lunate (B3, C1a and C1b, C2), with preservation of the radioscaphoid articulation.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: SCAPHOCAPITATE FUSION

Via a dorsal approach, the scaphoid/capitate articulation is exposed and denuded to bleeding bone. The scaphoid should be extended so that the load is preferentially transferred through the radial column. Thumb pressure on the scaphoid tubercle and index finger pressure on the Lister tubercle will achieve the proper extension and ulnar deviation. Under fluoroscopic guidance, two guide wires are advanced across the scaphocapitate interval. The first wire is at the junction of the proximal and middle- thirds of the scaphoid and is inserted 60 degrees to the longitudinal axis of the wrist, as seen in the AP plane. A second wire is inserted 5 mm distal and parallel to the first, entering the scaphoid between the middle and distal thirds. Fluoroscopy should identify normal carpal alignment with scapholunate (SL) and scaphocapitate (SC) angles of 30 to 57 degrees and the capitolunate angle at 0 ± 15 degrees. Morsellized bone graft is added to the fusion site and then cannulated screws are advanced over the wires.

The SC and STT fusion both increase the loading on the radial column and can lead to radioscaphoid arthritis. We do not excise the lunate. Lunate excision allows ulnar translocation of the carpus so that the scaphoid can be perched on or cross the sagittal (scapholunate) ridge of the radius. This may increase the risk of radioscaphoid arthritis by deepening the scaphoid facet. The addition of a ball of palmaris longus tendon does not prevent this translocation.

Other options. Less mainstream options include the radioscapholunate (RSL) fusion and Graner’s procedure.

Radioscapholunate fusion. Somewhat counterintuitively, in the right patient, we have found the RSL fusion to be a reliable procedure with good long- term results when the lunate facet and the proximal lunate are nonfunctional. The prerequisites are intact distal lunate and proximal capitate articulations and competent RSC ligament to maintain stability (Fig. 16.85). A coronal fracture extending into the distal lunate articular surface would be a contraindication for this procedure.

![[Fig.16.85.png]]
> **Fig. 16.85** RSL fusion. A, A 13-year follow-up. Hardware (staples) have been removed but a functional lunocapitate joint remains. B, At 15 years’ follow-up. There has been progression of lunocapitate degeneration, but no salvage procedure has been required. These cases were performed prior to our preference of excising the distal third scaphoid and triquetrum. (Copyright © Dr. Gregory Bain.)

Excellent pain relief, satisfaction, and function have been maintained for 10 years in 4 of our patients treated with RSL fusion. The flexion–extension arc is limited (mean of 72 degrees), but midcarpal and dart thrower’s motion is preserved. None of the patients developed nonunion or required additional salvage procedures.

Graner’s procedure (lunarectomy and capitate lengthening). The Graner’s procedure may prove to be an important addition to manage the younger high- demand patient (<40 years) with a nonreconstructible lunate but intact lunate facet and proximal capitate. The Graner’s procedure involves excising the diseased lunate, osteotomizing the capitate, and translating the capitate articular surface proximally.The articular surfaces of the adjacent scaphoid and hamate are also denuded. An interposition iliac crest graft is then added to the lengthened capitated to create a large fusion mass with the scaphoid and hamate. The capitate and remainder of the carpus are stabilized with a longitudinal screw and transverse Kirschner wires (Fig. 16.86). An alternative method is to perform the capitate lengthening via callus distraction using a dynamic external fixateur.

![[Fig.16.86.png]]
> **Fig. 16.86** Graner procedure of lunate excision (A) capitate osteotomy (B) with iliac crest interposition graft (B). C and D, The osteotomy site is stabilized with a headless compression screw and Kirschner wires. (A and B, Copyright © Gregory Bain. C and D, courtesy of Dr. Diego Fernandez, Switzerland.)

In contrast to the PRC, the Graner’s procedure maintains carpal height and distributes the articular load to both the radial and central columns.

Wrist salvage procedure. A salvage procedure is required when the patient has symptomatic advanced arthritic changes involving both the radiocarpal and midcarpal joints. This has previously been termed *Kienböck disease advance collapse (KDAC)* . The options are wrist arthrodesis and arthroplasty.

Total wrist arthrodesis. The total wrist fusion is the most commonly used salvage procedure and has reliable long- term results, which is especially important for young and active patients.

### AUTHOR’S PREFERRED METHOD OF TREATMENT: TOTAL WRIST ARTHRODESIS

Via a dorsal approach the lunate is assessed, and if severely necrotic or collapsed, excised and the triquetrum rotated into the space. The primary fusion mass is between the radius, proximal scaphoid, remaining lunate, and capitate. The STT joint is spared unless grossly arthritis. We use a nonspanning plate extending to the distal carpal row with the wrist in 10 to 15 degrees extension. The third carpometacarpal joint is left intact to avoid problems with nonunion and plate complications.Postoperatively, the wrist can be rapidly mobilized since the plate provides stability.

The wrist should not be loaded, however, until the radiographs demonstrate union typically by 3 months.

Wrist arthroplasty. There are many different types of arthroplasty, including total wrist arthroplasty, hemiarthroplasty, interposition arthroplasty, and lunate replacement arthroplasty. To date, wrist arthroplasty procedures are reserved for the lower demand patient. All of these arthroplasties have very limited use in Kienböck disease. However, newer designs may hopefully change this approach.

The Silastic lunate replacement was previously used, but the shear forces lead to silicon synovitis and implant failure. The pyrocarbon lunate replacement has some appeal, but wrist and prosthesis stability are still issues, which must be resolved before arthroplasties can be more widely used for Kienböck disease.

**Concluding comments.** The management of Kienböck disease continues to evolve thanks to considerable advances in imaging and arthroscopic joint and bone assessment. The Lichtman and Bain algorithm has consolidated many of the concepts developed in the last 2 to 3 decades. The lack of quality studies on the natural history of Kienböck disease is a challenge.

#### Preiser Disease

In 1910, Preiser described a rarefying osteitis of the scaphoid that he distinguished from a scaphoid fracture. He compared the condition with Kienböck disease and thought that the causes were similar. However, recently Kallen and Strackee have called into question Georg Preiser’s original observations. They recovered the images of the five cases he described and showed that they all had fractures but did not have AVN.

There are two main classification systems for Preiser disease. One is the Herbert- Lanzetta classification. Stage I represents normal radiographs but a positive bone scan, stage II an increased density of the proximal pole and generalized osteoporosis, stage III fragmentation of the proximal pole with or without a pathologic fracture, and stage IV a pattern of carpal collapse and osteoarthritis. The other is the Kalainov system which describes type 1, whole bone, and type 2, partial bone, patterns of Preiser disease. Type I has the propensity to fragmentation and collapse, no matter the treatment. Type 2 patterns usually maintain their architecture, with the same types of treatment.

The cause has been related to collagen vascular disease, steroid therapy, repetitive trauma, or idiopathic conditions. Clinical findings are related to local pain and tenderness. Radiographically, involved areas are typically sclerotic, and there is often fragmentation of the proximal articular surfaces. CT and MRI may be helpful to define the changes. This condition can present in children.

Treatment of Preiser disease has not been standardized. Nonoperative symptomatic management, joint debridement, silicone replacement arthroplasty, and electrical stimulation have all been tried, with mixed results. A recent report of 8 cases treated nonoperatively with oral pain medications, immobilization, and rest showed that subjective and objective outcomes were not improved and radiographs continued to deteriorate, with all cases eventually needing surgery. Reversedflow vascularized bundle implantation has been performed in a limited number of cases with variable success. Vascularized bone grafting may also be considered, but results have been variable and revascularization has been incomplete. Hayashi and associates reported on decreased but continued pain after closing wedge radial osteotomy. Tomori and colleagues reported on better results with closing radial wedge osteotomy in a small series of seven patients. Postoperatively, five patients had no pain, two had mild pain. Range of motion was maintained and grip strength improved. The success of scaphoid- preserving procedures may be compromised by chronic degenerative changes that are often present in the carpus at the time of reconstruction, particularly at the radial styloid and radioscaphoid articulation; this makes painfree motion following successful salvage difficult. Furthermore, there is often insufficient satisfactory cortical bone into which a graft can be inserted, as the surface is too fragmented. Therefore, if revascularization is to be considered, the patient should also consent to scaphoid excision and midcarpal arthrodesis or PRC, should vascularized grafting prove technically impossible. If revascularization is attempted and proves unsuccessful, scaphoid excision combined with either midcarpal arthrodesis or PRC may be appropriate for persistently symptomatic cases. Given the lack of reliable treatment options, in the absence of severe pain and disability, a conservative approach is probably warranted and should be discussed with the patient.

#### Avascular Necrosis of the Capitate

The capitate is at risk for AVN because the proximal pole is essentially an intraarticular structure. The retrograde intraosseous blood flow to the capitate is similar to that in the scaphoid, which makes the proximal pole of the capitate prone to AVN at its waist. Dorsal and palmar vessels are present but vary considerably. Additional causes of AVN include vibration exposure, steroid use, repetitive wrist extension, and ligamentous laxity.

Attempts to promote revascularization with bone grafting have had mixed results. There are no long- term follow- up reports on AVN. AVN has been reported infrequently in isolated capitate fractures but is more common in higher- energy fractures, particularly when the capitate is rotated.

Usami and colleagues reported on one case of vascularized second metacarpal bone graft with relief of wrist pain and maintained range of motion.

## Case Studies

### CASE STUDY 16.1 Scaphoid Nonunion

A 27-year-old right-hand–dominant man had sustained a proximal pole scaphoid fracture 2 years prior to presentation. He was initially casted, but the scaphoid failed to heal. One year later he had undergone grafting with the 1,2 intercompartmental vascularized bone flap. Again the bone failed to unite. He presents now with a very small proximal pole scaphoid nonunion (eFigs. 16.1 and 16.2) that has caused pain with grasping.

![[eFig.16.1.png]]
> **eFig. 16.1** Proximal pole nonunion, PA radiograph.

![[eFig.16.2.png]]
> **eFig. 16.2** Proximal pole nonunion, lateral radiograph.

In order to preserve his scaphoid, he underwent free medial femoral trochlear osteochondral flap reconstruction. The screw was removed as well as a subtotal proximal scaphoid excision was performed to the level of the scaphoid waist (eFig. 16.3).

![[eFig.16.3.png]]
> **eFig. 16.3** Subtotal proximal scaphoid excision.

The convex cartilage of the proximal most portion of the medial femoral trochlea was harvested in continuity with the periosteal transverse branch of the descending geniculate artery (eFigs. 16.4 and 16.5).

![[eFig.16.4.png]]
> **eFig. 16.4** Convex cartilage of medial femoral trochlea harvesting.

![[eFig.16.5.png]]
> **eFig. 16.5** Convex cartilage of medial femoral trochlea harvesting.

The cartilage surface was used to recreate the greater curvature of the proximal scaphoid. The flap was secured to the distal pole via cannulated screw fixation. The vascular pedicle was anastomosed to the radial artery in the snuffbox in an end-to-side fashion. X-rays at 4 weeks demonstrated good alignment and preservation of normal scapholunate alignment (eFigs. 16.6 and 16.7). X-rays 1 year following operation reveal excellent recapitulation of the scaphoid form (eFig. 16.8).

![[eFig.16.6.png]]
> **eFig. 16.6** Four weeks postoperative, lateral radiograph.

![[eFig.16.7.png]]
> **eFig. 16.7** Four weeks postoperative, PA radiograph.

![[eFig.16.8.png]]
> **eFig. 16.8** One year postoperative, PA radiograph.

*James P. Higgins, MD*
*Chief, Curtis National Hand Center, Union Memorial Hospital, Baltimore, Maryland*
